2489. THOMAS HARVEY and THOMAS BLACKMAN were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Thomas Crisp, about one in the night of the 11th of Oct., at Bermondsey, with intent to steal,
MR. PARNELL conducted the Prosecution
WILLIAM NOAKES ( policeman, M 104.) I know Mr. Crisp's premises in Bermondsey-wall, in the parish of Bermondsey—I was on duty there on Tuesday morning, the 12th of Oct., about one o'clock, or a little later, I tried the gate of the premises, it opens into a yard which leads to the back of the counting-house—I saw a pair of steps places against the counting-house window, which was open—I went up the steps, looked in, and saw everything strewed about in confusion—a cheat of drawers was drawn out close to the door, so that nobody could get in at the door—it is a door leading from the counting-house to the dwelling house—I came down the steps, turned on my light, and saw the two prisoners lying down on a hen-coop beneath some shrubs, striving to conceal themselves—I had known them both well before—they both jumped up and ran into another corner of the yard—I turned my light on them both again—they found they could not get away, and I secured Harvey—Blackman escaped—I called him by name as he was getting over the fence—Harvey said, "It is not much of the case, the window was open before I came"—I took him to the station-house—I afterwards examined the counting-house window, and found there had been some small instrument put underneath to force it up—there were no sash-lines to it, so that it required some force to get it up—next morning I searched the yard and found this letter lying in the direction in which Blackman had escaped over the fence—it was within a few yards of the fence.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. The moment you turned your light on they ran away to another part of the yard? A. Yes—the window was up as high as it could go.
THOMAS CRISP. I live in Mill-street, Bermondsey, and am a shipwright—my dwelling-house adjoins the counting-house—they are not under the same roof, but it all adjoins—there is a door from one to other—the officer knocked me up on the morning of the 12th of Oct. at half-past one—I went to the street-door and spoke to him, and then went to get into the counting-house, but the door was barricaded up with a chest of drawers, so that I could not get it—the chest of drawers had been in their place the night before, close by the door—I had been in the room at a quarter to seven in the evening before, and left my son there—he us here—when I found I could not get in that way, I came out at the street-door, called up my foreman, and ultimately went into the counting-house by the window—I found it in a state of confusion—some one had been there—this letter produced is a letter from one of my captains from Calcutta—it had been in one of the drawers with others.
Cross-examined. Q. There was a pair of steps against the window! A. Yes, and the lower sash of the window was open—the counting-house is a part of the dwelling-house—the roof is a lean-to—there is a door from the dwelling-house to the counting-house—I have one entrance to the counting-house from the yard, and another from the private house.
THOMAS WILLIAM CRISP. I am the prosecutor's son—I was in the counting-house on Monday night, the 11th, after my father—I left at seven o'clock—I locked it up—the window was shut down and fastened.
Cross-examined. Q Is the dwelling-house yours or your father's? A. My father's—I am not in partnership with him—my brother is—he lives in East-lane, some distance from there—my father, mother, and the servants live in the house—my brother does not.
JAMES THARM. I live with my mother in Providence-buildings, Bermondsey. I know both the prisoners—they used to lodge with my mother—they did not lodge on the night of the burglary, the 11th of Oct.—they did the night before—I saw them on the Monday night, between seven and eight o'clock, at the Ship A-ground, within 150 yards of Mr. Crisp's, and I saw them there about twelve, and asked them if they were coming home—one of them said,:No"—next morning about seven o'clock I saw Blackman by my bedside at my mother's—that was after the police had been to my mother's—he asked me what had been the matter—I told him that Harvey was taken into custody, I was told, for breaking into Mr. Crisp's premises—he did not say anything, but walked down stairs, and went away—he never came back to my mother's after that—I saw no more of him till he was in custody.
Cross-examined. Q. how do you get your living? A. By work when I can get it—that is not very seldom—I was at work about a month or two before this—I can play the violo—I do not get my living by it at public-houses—I do play occasionally—I was not playing the fiddle at the Ship a-ground—I was at the Tailor's Arms, in Shad Thames on this night—I know a person named Tracey—I saw the prisoners and Tracey at the Ship a-ground, all three of them during the evening—Tracey and Blackman both came to me next morning—Tracey came first, about half an hour before Blackman—I swear Blackman came—I swear all three were at the Ship a-ground—I was in custody myself once for the month, for being out of a night—my mother gave me into custody—my brother came home tipsy one evening, and broke
my violin, and I came out and swore I would not come into the house again, my mother sent the policeman after me, and Mr. Cottingham sent me to prison because I would not come home—they said nothing else against me—I was never given into custody for anything else—I have been at a station once besides for being out of a night—I last saw Tracey on the morning he came to my bedside—I had not known him more than nine months before.
WILLIAM FORDHAM ( policeman, M 262.) In consequence of information I looked for Blackman, and on the 18th of Oct. found him in the Old Kent-road, he was on one side, and I on the other—as soon as he saw me he ran away—I pursued him for about half an hour, came up to him, and stopped him in the New-road which leads into the Deptford-road, and into Bermondsey—he asked what I wanted him for—I told him for being concerned in a burglary at Mr. Crisp's—he said he knew nothing about it.
Harvey's Defence. I was out, and went down there, and thought it was a shed to sleep in.
HARVEY — GUILTY. Aged 19.
BLACKMAN — GUILTY. Aged 21.
Confined Fifteen Months.
Before Lord Chief Baron Pollock.
[trialAccount t18471025-2490]
2490. LOUISA VINCENT was indicted for feloniously cutting and wounding Thomas Soarston, on the left side of his head, with intent to do him grievous bodily harm.
THOMAS SOARSTON. I am a carpenter, and live in Kendall-street, New Cross, Deptford. I know the prisoner—I believe the child she has now in her arms to be mine—I have contributed to its maintenance ever since it has been born—I have allowed her 3 s. 6 d. a-week until I was had up at the Court, and then I was to pay half-a-crown a-week—I lived with her two years ago, for about eighteen months—I have left her nearly two years—the child is twelve months old next May—she would never keep away from me—she has been to my lodgings several times—the last time she came was a month ago yesterday, between eight and nine in the morning—she knocked at the door, and I answered her—she said I was the party she wished to see—I said, "Whatever you do be quiet"—she rushed into the kitchen, and began knocking me about as well as she was able—after that she was quiet, and I thought she was going away quietly—the child was with her—she put it down under the table, and began violently to assault me as far as laid in her power, after which the child was taken away by the neighbours—I thought she was then going away quietly, when she struck me with the poker—she had hit me with the rolling-pin previously, but I do not make any complaint about that—she struck me with the poker on the forehead—she said she should not mind being hanged for me—I cannot say the exact words she used.
Prisoner. He beat me with the rolling-pin, and I struck him in my own defence; he knocked me down; whilst on my back he put his left hand round me, and tried to strangle me; he took up the rolling-pin and nearly broke my arm; I was very much bruised. Witness. It was some considerable time after that she struck me with the poker—there were some blows exchanged on both sides with the rolling-pin—she first came between eight and nine—as near as I can recollect, she staid altogether about twenty minutes—she struck me first with getting the better of me—she is a powerful person.
Prisoner. I went for my money—I was without victuals four days. Witness.
She had three week's money due to her at the time—I did not hit her particularly hard—it was not harder on one side than the other—she hit me as hard as she could, and perhaps just in the moment of excitement I hit her as hard as I could—I did not have a surgeon to attend to the wound, but I have the mark of it.
WILLIAM MORCOMB. I heard the disturbance, but did not see the beginning of it.
SAMUEL COPPING ( policeman.) About nine o'clock, on the 25th of Sept. the prosecutor came to me, I looked at his head where he was cut—blood was flowing down each side of his cheek—I found a large cut on his forehead—I put my finger on it, and pressed the wound down, and it did not come up again—I told him to go to the doctor—he did not go—the prisoner was not there then—she was in a beer shop opposite—I went there to her, and told her she was my prisoner for cutting a man's head open with a poker—she said, "It serves him right, I mean it"—she said nothing about being beaten with the rolling-pin, or about any scuffle between them.
Prisoner. I told him I had been very much beaten—I did not know whether I could carry the child to the station for my arms were so bruised about.
Witness. When she got about a mile towards the station, she said, "My arm is bruised, and I can hardly carry the child, "but when I took her she never said a word—she pulled up her sleeve at the station, but I could not see any bruises.
Prisoner. A woman named Martha Davis saw the bruises about me, and promised to bring my bonnet here to show.
( The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that the prosecutor, after premissing her marriage, had induced her to live with him, telling her that his wife was transported; that after some time he wished her to obtain money by prostitution, which she refusing he deserted her, agreeing to allow her something for the child; that she went to him for the money, when he knocked her down and ill-used her, and all she did was in her own defence.)
MARTHA DAVIS. I remember the day when the prisoner was apprehended on this charge—I was with her the whole of the day—I saw her when she came back from the prosecutor's—she was bruised very much on her arm and sides—she showed me the bruises—she appeared to have been beaten.
THOMAS SOARSTON re-examined. I am a married man.
NOT GUILTY.
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.
[trialAccount t18471025-2491]
2491. CHARLES FRANK COMBE was indicted for a robbery on Charles Soper, putting him in fear, and stealing from his person, and against his will, 2 sovereigns, his moneys, and beating, striking, and using other personal violence to him.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
CHARLES SOPER. I am clerk to Mr. Knapp, a barrister, and live at No. 1, Walcot-square, Lambeth. On Saturday night, the 9th of Oct., I was at the Cornwall Arms, Cornwall-road, and saw the prisoner there—I asked if he would go of an errand for me to Princes-street—he went, and returned in twenty minutes—there were several persons there besides the prisoner, but he was drinking with two in particular—I had to change half-a-sovereign for the purpose of his going on the errand—he saw me do that—I paid for two pints of ale, which he drank the best part of—I gave him 6 d. for going on the errand—he said I had given the landlord a sovereign instead of a half-sovereign, and told me to look—I put my hand into my pocket, found my
money all right, and said, "No"—I had about 2 l. 10 s., and a few halfpence—that was at about eight o'clock—I had gone there purposely to meet a friend—I left the house about a quarter to nine, or nine o'clock, and went towards my friend's house, where I had sent the prisoner with the message—(he had returned with word that he would be at home in an hour, and that was my object in waiting at the public-house)—as I was going along I put my hand in my pocket, to see that I had my cash all right, and I put my gold in one pocket, and my silver in the other—it was all right—I went to my friend's house, stood talking there five or ten minutes, and then went towards Broadwall and St. Saviour's workhouse, in a direct line to my own home—it rained very fast—the prisoner came up to me close by St. Saviour's workhouse—he was alone—he walked by my side, and thinking he was one of Mr. Maudslay's men, I introduced the subject of the late fire—I am positive it was the prisoner—he hit me in the mouth with his fist, thrust, his hand into my pocket, and tripped me up, by putting his leg between mine—whether he took my money while I was falling or not I do not know, but my gold was gone directly I got up, and my pocket turned inside out—when I got up I saw the prisoner running under the lamp—I called out, "Stop thief!" as well as I could, but I had not much breath in my body—I had never been to this public-house but once before, and that was with my friend.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. Who was this friend? A. Mr. Edgcombe, the owner of the house—I had left chambers at four o'clock—I had been drinking—I had paid for four pints of pale ale between four and seven o'clock—I had no spirits—I had a pint of half-and-half, with some bread and cheese—I have a perfect recollection of everything that took place—I was quite sober when the prisoner knocked me down—I had nothing but the two pints of ale in the Cornwall Arms, and the prisoner drank nearly the whole of that—I was there about three quarters of an hour, or an hour altogether—I was not drinking with any one but the prisoner, neither did I speak to any person—the landlord turned the prisoner out of the parlour—I had not invited him in—he followed me in and sat by my side, and the landlord requested him to go out—I had never seen him before—he followed me from the Cornwall Arms—I cannot say I was sober—I was certainly not drunk—I was sober when he knocked me down—it sobered me—the perspiration ran from me—this occurred not a quarter of a mile from my friend's house—I had not been talking twenty minutes there—I was talking to Sarah Blake at my friend's house for about the minutes—she was no friend of mine—I had never seen her before—I walked from the Cornwall Arms to Princes-street.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You sent a man with a message whom you say was the prisoner? A. Yes—I am sure it was the person I sent with the message that knocked me down—I saw him by the light at St. Saviour's workhouse.
JOHN PRETTY. I am landlord of the Cornwall Arms. On Saturday, the 9th of Oct., Soper was at my house eight o'clock—I saw the prisoner there—I had seen him several times before, and am confident he is the man—I saw Soper send him on a message to Mr. Edgcombe, and saw him return—Soper went into the parlour, and the prisoner followed him—I followed them into the parlour, not thinking that the prisoner was properly dressed for my parlour.
Cross-examined. Q. I suppose parlour is frequented by respectable
tradesmen? A. Yes, and I did not think him fit company—he did not go in by Soper's invitation, he followed him in—Soper had two pints of six-penny ale—I ordered the prisoner out of the parlour—he asked Soper to come with him, and he followed him out of the parlour—Soper was the worse for liquor—they both left my house in company together.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did Soper appear to be drunk? A. Not at all—I consider he was quite capable of taking care of himself—a gentleman came with him in a cab to my house, and they had four pints of ale between them.
SARAH BLAKE. I am a monthly nurse—Mr. Soper sent me a message by a young man—the prisoner is the person—I have no doubt about him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did Soper afterwards come himself? A. Yes—he first came in a cab with a gentleman, and about a quarter of an hour afterwards he came again—he was the worse for liquor when he first came, he was not so bad the second time.
JOHN HERRINGTON. I am a furrier. On this Saturday night I heard a cry of "Stop thief!" and saw somebody running away—I could not swear to the person—I cannot say whether it was the prisoner or not—the person had on a cap a velveteen coat, and was about five feet four in height.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you not say he was a stout man? A. No, I said he was neither stout nor thin—I did not see his face.
WILLIAM ROMAIN ( policeman, L 38.) I took the prisoner into custody—he was in bed—I told him it was on suspicion of knocking a gentleman down, and robbing him on Saturday night, the 9th of Oct.—he denied the charge—he admitted that he was in company with a gentleman at the Corn-wall Arms, and that he had accompanied him into Princes-street, Stamford-street, but denied that he was the person that knocked him down.
Cross-examined. Q. What time did you take him? A. Between two and three in the morning, at his own lodging.
JOHN PRETTY re-examined. The prisoner was dressed in a cap and velveteen coat when at my house.
(Elijah Harvey, floorcloth beater, Lambeth, and William Kay, stater, 6, Upper Marsh-street, Lambeth, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 23.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18471025-2492]
2492. WILLIAM PIM, HENRY CLARK, JOSEPH EWINGTON , and THOMAS ALLEN , were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Pim, about twelve in the night of the 11th of Oct., at Lambeth, with intent to steal, and stealing therein 1 coat, value 5 s.; 1 waistcoat, 2 s.; 1 handkerchier, 1 s.; and a variety of tools his property; 1 plough, 6 d.; 3 saws, 5 s.; and other tools, of Thomas Seatle; Pim and Allen having been before convicted of felony; to which
PIM pleaded GUILTY. Aged 24.— Transported for Ten Years.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN PIM. I am a builder, and live at 6, White Hart-place, Kennington-lane, in the parish of Lambeth—it is my dwelling-house—the prisoner Pim is my son—he worked for me, and lived in my house up to the 11th of Oct.—I went to bed that night about nine o'clock—I got up next morning about a quarter or ten minutes before six, came down stairs, opened my back-parlour shutters, and saw a ladder standing against the back stop—I then opened my front shop, and on returning to the back, saw my workman's tool-chest broken open—the lock had been cut away by some instrument—I do not know how
the house had been entered—some of my workman's best tools were gone from the chest, and a basket of my tools also, worth about 1 l.—they were taken from the kitchen and workshop—I went to my son's bedroom, he was not there, and had not been in bed—I went into the front room first floor, found a cupboard broken open, and some spirits and wine gone—an attempt had been made to break open the door of my back-shop or iron-foundry, but that had not succeeded—I went into the kitchen, and there saw three chairs placed, and two empty bottles on the table—the thieves had been regaling themselves—here are a quantity of tools produced, some of which are mine, and some my workman's.
Cross-examined by MR. BALDWIN. Q. Does the back shop adjoin the dwelling-house? A. There is about two yards' separation between my dwelling-house and the back shop—nobody lives in the back shop—there was no reason why my son should not have slept at home that night—I should say there were three persons engaged in this, from the three chairs which I found placed in the kitchen.
THOMAS SEATLE. I am assistant to Mr. Pin—I left my tools safe on Monday, the 11th of Oct., and missed them next morning—these now produced are them—they are worth 1 l. 17 s. 10 d.
THOMAS STEWART ROGERS ( police-inspector L.) On Tuesday morning, the 12th of Oct., about a quarter to one, I was standing at Kennington-cross, about seventy yards from Mr. Pim's house, and saw a man cross from the direction of Mr. Pim's, across Walcot-place, with a basket of tools on his back—I believe it was the prisoner Clark—he was staggering, I thought he was drunk.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know Clark? A. No—I believe him to be the person I saw, and pointed him out next day.
HENRY EVANS. I assist my father, who is a publican, at Lambeth. Last Monday week, about half-past twelve o'clock at night, Clark came to our house for half-a-pint of beer—I served him—he had a basket of tools over his arm, and a hand-saw in his hand—he asked if he might leave them till the morning—I said yes, and I put them in the kitchen—he was a little drunk—I gave the tools to the policeman in the morning.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you mean that he was a little intoxicated or very drunk? A. Very drunk—I did know him before, except by coming to the house once or twice.
THOMAS HENRY EVANS. I am last witness' father—I know Clark—I saw him in my skittle-ground on the Tuesday afternoon, about twelve o'clock—I asked what he wanted—she said a basket—I said what basket—he said a basket of tools that he had left the over-night with my eldest son—I said, "Tools!"—he said, "Carpenter's tools"—I said, "Indeed, then I want you," and I collared him, and gave him into custody—he said he was innocent, he knew nothing about it.
ABRAHAM ABRAHAMS. I am a broker, in Vere-street, Clare-market. On this Tuesday, about twelve o'clock, Pim, Ewington, and Allen came to my shop—Allen and Ewington had each a basket of tools—they asked if I would buy any tools—I said if I knew where they came from I would, if they would suit me—they directly walked to the back of shop into another room, and began to throw them out on the table—I said they would not suit me at any price, unless I knew where they came from—Allen said, "You may depend upon it they are all right, I have been known in this neighbourhood for a length of time, and I have pledged some of them in my name"—I did not like their proceedings, and said they would not suit me—they said I might have them for 1 l., and then 10 s. I told them to pack them up and go—they
were quite drunk, and were taking liberties with the servant girl—one of them whispered in my ear, "Give me a bob, you can have them for a bull "—I understood that to mean, that if I gave them 1 s. I could have them for a crown—Phillips, the beadle, came in, and Allen immediately said, "Phillips knows me, you can buy them now with safety"—I would not—Phillips went away, and they packed the tools up—in going out of doors Ewington hit me in the face, and said I was a muff, and would not take a pound if it was given me—they took the tools away with them—these are the same sort of tools—this stock they pointed out to me as being worth a great deal. and also a brown great coat one of them had on—I believe it was Ewington—this is the coat.
JOHN PIM re-examined. These are my tools, and these my man's—they are not the same I saw just now—this great coat is mine, and was in my house on the night in question.
JOHN PHILLIPS. I am street-keeper at St. Clement Danes. On the Tuesday in question went into Abrahams' shop, and saw Pim, Ewington, and Allen there, and a quantity of tools lying on the back-parlour table—Allen said, "Here is the beadle, he knows me well, why don't you buy them?"—I went out for a policeman—I afterwards took them in a cab to the station. and on the way Clark was brought up to the cab by another constable—they were taken in a public-house next door to Abrahams'—I produce the two baskets of tools, and the great coat which I found on Ewington's person, under his jacket I found this waistcoat, belonging to Mr. Pim, and this handkerchief in the coat-pocket.
Ewington's Defence. I was coming by Mr. Pim's about five o'clock on Tuesday morning, and met Mr. Pim's son, with a basket of tools, a great coat, and a waistcoat, which he asked me to help him carry; I did so; he told me to put the coat on, and said it was his own; I put it on, and he gave me the waistcoat to button underneath my own, which I did; I helped him carry the things to where he was going, and that is all I know about it.
Allen's Defence. I have worked in Covent-garden Market for four years; on Tuesday morning, at half-past eight, my wife brought me my breakfast, and about half-past nine I went into a public-house at the corner of Wild-street; Pim and Ewington were there drinking; I never saw them before; Pim offered me a pipe of tobacco; we got into conversation, and he said, "I have been on the spree all night, drinking wine and gin;" Ewington said the same; they said they had no money, and I stood a pint of porter; Pim pulled a saw out of a basket, and asked my wife to pawn it for 1 s.; I said I would rather do it myself, and I went and pledged it; I forgot the name he told me, and gave my own and my address, No. 28, Portpool-lane, where I have lived two years and nine months; I gave Pim the ticket and money.
Pim. What Allen has stated is quite true; he is innocent.
JAMES GREEN. I produce a certificate of Allen's former conviction—( read—Convicted March, 1843, of housebreaking, and confined one year)—he is the person.
(Clark received a good character.)
CLARK — GUILTY. Aged 31.
EWINGTON — GUILTY. Aged 24.
Confined One Year.
ALLEN — GUILTY. * Aged 23.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18471025-2493]
2493. JOHN MARSON was indicted for unlawfully obtaining from Henry Doulton 50 earthen pipes, by false pretences; 2nd COUNT, for conspiring with others to obtain the said goods, with intent to defraud the said Henry Doulton and another of the same
MESSRS. BALLANTINE and PARNELL conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY DOULTON. I carry on business in partnership with John Doulton, in High-street, Lambeth. On the 24th of Aug., about three o'clock, in consequence of something I heard, I went to the Victoria-road, Kensington, and saw the prisoner there—I told him I understood he had called at our counting-house, and required a quantity of pipes for a sewer he was about to build in that neighbourhood—he said he did, he should want 600 of 9-inch pipes—I said, "I understand that the ground landlord of the property will be here, and will give me a guarantee for the payment"—he said he was there, and introduced me to a gentleman who he called Richard Day, Esq., of Eltham, who he said banked at Herries, Farquhar, and Co.—I and the prisoner walked on together, and left Mr. Day there—we went to Kensington new workhouse—it was not quite finished then—there was a sewer being made in the neighbourhood—the men were at work at it—he said, "You see that they are getting on with it, and if you don't send the first thing in the morning, they will be standing still"—he was almost within hearing of the men, I think they could have heard him, and he pointed to them—after that I went back to Mr. Day, and went to a public-house with him, and he gave me a guarantee for the payment, which I produce—it was given in the prisoner's presence—the next morning he sent a man, saying the works were standing still, and in consequence of what had passed the previous day, I sent fifty stone pipes, worth 5 l. 12 s. 6 d.—a day or two after that, in consequence of something that my man told me, I went to look after the prisoner—he was not at home—he had left an address in my counting-house—I found the pipes were at Marlborough-road, Chelsea, at the premises of Messrs. Humphrey and Thirak, on some premises that were building about a mile and a half from the sewer—I have never been paid—I made repeated applications, and wrote to Mr. Day, and he said when the order was completed, he would see that Mr. Marson paid it.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. You applied to Mr. Day more than once? A. I did—the prisoner gave his address Henry-street, Marlborough-road, Chelsea—I called there after the goods were sent, not before—I did not see him—I found that he lived there—it was a miserable house—a man that lived in such a house would not take such a contract—the guarantee was given in a public-house—the prisoner introduced me to Mr. Day as being the ground landlord—he had left word at my counting-house that the ground landlord would give me a guarantee—I should have parted with the pipes without the guarantee, under the circumstances, because when he said, in the presence of these men, he was building the sewer, I thought it sufficient guarantee to his respectability, that the guardians of the union should let out the work to him—I took the guarantee as an additional security—I did not require it—it was offered me, and I thought it right to accept it—I did not positively require it—I told him that I understood Mr. Day was willing to give it, and I was willing to receive it, and he gave it me—I did not mention it before I spoke of the price of the pipes—I had bargained about the pipes before he wrote the guarantee, but not I think before he mentioned that Mr. Day was willing to give it—I should, under the circumstances, have given the 70 l. worth of pipes without receiving the guarantee, from the gentleman representing himself as the ground landlord—I really cannot say when I
applied to Mr. Day—it was a week or so afterwards—he said if I fulfilled the contract, he would comply with the guarantee—I did not see him—I never saw him after he gave me the guarantee—I saw the pipes about a week afterwards—I refused to send any more than the first load.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did you find out that Mr. Day was a gentleman of fortune, living at Eltham? A. No, I did not go down to Eltham.
ROBERT STIMPSON. I am carman to Messrs. Doulton—I took these pipes to Victoria-road, near the new workhouse—I did not find the prisoner there when I first went—I found the sewer going on there; I then went back to the Victoria-road, and saw the prisoner—he said, "Where have you been to; you have been to the wrong place"—I said, "No, I have not; I have been to the sewer, near the New Union"—I told him, "I have heard you are not doing the sewer there, or at Victoria-road; Mr. Williams told me, that he did not know you, and he said he had the contract to do both sewers"—he said, "Mr. Williams dies not want to know me; for I took the job out of his hands"—I then left the pipes—I have not seen him since—I got him to sign on the bottom of my bill that he had received them—I communicated my suspicions to my master.
Cross-examined. Q. You had been to where these sewers were being made that day? A. Yes; I knew before I delivered the pipes, that he was not the contractor for those sewers—that was only from what I heard—I could not make it out, when he told me one story, and the other another.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. And not being able to make it out one way or the other, you did what your master told you? A. Yes.
ROBERT COCKBURN. I am a stone-mason, at 14, Marlborough-street, Chelsea. One Friday, at the latter end of Aug., the prisoner came to me, and asked me if I knew anybody that was likely to buy some stone pipes—I told him I did not know anybody more likely than Mr. Thirsk, as he was the only person I knew that used them—the prisoner asked me if I would go to Mr. Thirsk and ask him to buy the pipes—I said I would not, and told him to go himself—I afterwards removed them in a cart, from Kensington to Messrs. Humphreys and Thirsk's—the prisoner employed me to do it—I saw him paid 4 l. 19 s. 6 d. by Mr. Thirsk himself.
EDWARD THIRSK. I am a builder, and live at No. 11, Halsey-terrace, Cadogan-terrace, Chelsea—Cockburn brought the prisoner to me—the prisoner asked me to buy fifty stone pipes for 5 l.—I gave him 4 l. 19 s. 6 d.—I could buy plenty at that price—it was a fair price.
SAMUEL WILLIAMS. I am a carman and building contractor, at 56, Clifton-street, Shepherd's-bush—I had the contract for building the new sewers, at Kensington New Union Workhouse, and nobody besides my brother and myself had any contract—it was for all the sewers that were to be made in the neighbourhood—I never gave the prisoner any authority at any time, to order any pipes for the sewers—I had no dispute with him about who was to have the contract—he never had anything to do with the contract—Mr. Gallott is the ground-landlord of the field where the pipes go across—Mr. Day, of Eltham, has nothing to do with it to my knowledge.
WILLIAM ATLEE ( policeman.) I apprehended the prisoner—I told him I wanted him for obtaining some pipes from Mr. Doulton—he said, very well, he would go with me—I have been down to Eltham, in Kent, and have made inquiries for Mr. Day, a gentleman of fortune, living there—I found no gentleman of fortune living there of that name—there was a man named Day, lodging in a back-room at one Hennell's, a shoemaker's—I have been
searching for a Day and Hennell both since then, and have found neither—after I paid then the visit, they dissppeared—it was quite a small shop.
Cross-examined. Q. When did you go? A. I went on six or seven occasions—I have not seen Day at all—I found he had been living there—the first time I went was I think on the 9th of Oct.
GUILTY on 1st Count. Aged 38.— Confined One Year. ( See next case.)
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
[trialAccount t18471025-2494]
2494. WILLIAM LEE was indicted for unlawfully obtaining, by false pretences, 480 lbs. weight of white of white lead, value 6 l.; 12 gallons of varnish, 8 l.; and other articles, the goods of Augustus Ratcliffe, the younger; with intent to cheat and defraud him of the same.
MR. PARNELL conducted the Prosecution.
AUGUSTUS RATCLIFFE, JUN. I am a window-glass and lead-merchant, in Chichester-place, Gray's-Inn-road. On the 7th of Aug. the prisoner came to my shop, and said he wanted to see Mr. Soar, my traveller—I said, "He is not in; shall I do as well"—he said he knew Mr. Soar from his calling on his former master, Mr. O. Donnel, who lived at Chelsea; that he had left him, through his doing some business for Mr. Richard Day, and had now started on his own account; that he had got some houses now to paint, I think he said five, and he wanted a lot of colour and turps to do them; would I supply them—he presented to me this bill ( produced)—he left it with me—it is drawn by Marson upon Richard Day, Esq., of Eltham—I said I would take the bill, if he would let me take the particulars, and let me inquire at the Bank if it was all right—I took the particulars of the bill and made inquiries; and in consequence saw him again about the 12th—he then gave me the order, which was for a quantity of white lead and other things—this is the invoice ( producing it), I took the bill—he said, "Be sure and send them over directly, because the workmen are standing still"—the bill is for 24 l. 1 s. 9 d.
COURT. Q. It was the bill that induced you to send the things? A Yes—I parted with a little varnish afterwards on another pretence.
MR. PARNELL. Q. How long after did you see him again? A. About the 24th—he then brought a small phial containing some varnish—he said that was part of the first quantity I had sold him, that he had tried it on some clean white paper, and found it completely spoilt the whole of it—it was too dark for it, and would I let him have a lot more, much lighter—I let him have it—there was no invoice for that—he said be sure and send them directly, as his men were standing still—I sent part of it by the Parcels' Delivery Company for the men to go on with—the bill of exchange was more than covered by the first order—the second time I trusted to his representation of having a good job, and also that he wanted it in exchange for this other varnish—he represented that he had five or six houses to paint, and had five or six men on—the whole of the varnish was worth about 5 l. 5 s.—part was sent by the Parcels' Delivery Company, and part the Tasker—I have seen it since at Mr. Scott's, an oilman's, in the Marlboro'-road, Chelsea—the bill was returned to me—I have never been able to get the money—I went to where the prisoner lived—it was a small sort of chandler's-shop, at Chelsea—I have been down to Eltham to look for Mr. Day—there were two or three policeman after him then, I could not find him at all—I found the varnish at Mr. Scott's, on the 12th or 13th of October, the day after we look the prisoner into custody—the cans are here—( produced).
Prisoner. Q. When I presented that bill to you, did not I inquire for Mr. Soar? A. Yes—you did not tell me to go to the bank and see if it was correct—I said I would go—the varnish is not in the same invoice as the other things—eight gallons were sent afterwards.
EDWARD SCOTT. I live in Marlboro'-road, Chelsea. On the 13th of Aug., about the middle of the day, Marson, who was tried just now, came to me with a broker, named Smith, in the Fulham-road—in consequence of what Marson said, I saw the prisoner that same day in Turk's-row, Chelsea, where he was living—I purchased of him number of goods, some of which are here, and have been identified by the prosecutor—here are two cans which contained paper varnish, for which I paid the prisoner 30 s.—he gave me a receipt for the money—I forget the date, I think it was after the 24th of Aug.—this is the receipt—it is dated the 1st of Sept., and is for 2 l. 4 s.—this includes some of the varnish—I had before that purchased oil and varnish of him to the amount of 7 l. 15 s.—I had not known him before.
JAMES TASKER. I am carman to the prosecutor—I delivered some varnish and brushes soon after the 24th of Aug., it might be four or five days after I delivered the first lot of goods, which was on the 13th of Aug.—I delivered them at Turk's-row, Chelsea—there were several persons there—when I first went I could not find the prisoner—once when I went, the prisoner said, had the varnish been there before, the rooms would have been varnished and dried, but he was not going to be humbugged by Mr. Ratcliffe, or anybody else, if he did not send it down, he must do something else with it, but did not say what.
JAMES MORRIS. I live in Turk's-row, Chelsea, opposite the prisoner. In the middle of Aug. I saw a great quantity of white lead and oil at the prisoner's—I was employed by him to roll it into the back yard—about a week afterwards, I saw some varnish brought there, and the prisoner employed me next day to take it to Mr. Scott's—I never saw the prisoner at work, he is a carpenter by trade, but I have seen him about Turk's-row all day long, backwards and forwards—I never saw any men employed by him, or say carts going from his premises—he has been living there nearly two years—he was at work seven or eight months ago, for a Mr. O'Donnell—I have not known him at work for anybody since—I am at work for a cowkeeper, and have been for the last nine years, on and off—my father is a pensioner.
CHARLES WALKER. I keep the house, 7, Turk's-row, Chelsea. The prisoner has lodged there upwards of two years—he did work for Mr. O'Donnell, and left him about three months ago—I do not think he has been in any employment since—I recollect the paint and varnish being brought to the house, it remained there two days—I saw the cart take it away the second evening—the prisoner hired the cart to take it to Mr. Scott's—none of it was sent to use at any house that I know of—the prisoner was not employed in painting or papering any houses at that time I know of—he was generally about the premises—I never saw any workmen come to him.
Prisoner's Defence. Marson brought this bill to me, and said he had taken one or two houses from Cockburn (who was a witness against him,) to paint, and asked me if I could get any credit—I said I did not know—he said, "Can you get anything on this bill?"—I said, "I do not understand anything about this bills"—he said, "If you can, I can get a bill, and a very good acceptance"—next day he brought me this bill—I took it to Walker, who said it was all right—I took it to Mr. Ratcliffe, and told him may business, that I had so and so to do, and had taken this bill, if he liked to take it in the way of ade—the thing was agreed on—I went with Marson to
Cockburn, and agreed to take 10 l. to paint the house, and when I found that everything was not exactly correct I would not have any more to do with it—I was made a tool of altogether—Day I never saw, but Marson told me he was a man of independent property.
ROBERT COCKBURN. Marson brought the prisoner to me as a painter, and I agreed to give Marson 10 l. for painting the house—the prisoner said he was a painter, and he was going to paint it and grain it likewise—I said I wanted to done directly, as I wanted to let it at Michaelmas.
CHARLES WALKER re-examined. I know Marson by sight—I have seen him at my house several times with the prisoner—the prisoner said that he and Marson were going into partnership in business, and he showed me a bill in which both names were.
GUILTY. — Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18471025-2494a]
2494. DAVID ABEL BROWN was indicted for bigamy.
WILLIAM BROWN. I am pensioner in Greenwich Hospital, the prisoner is my son. I was present at his marriage to Maria Stokes, to the best of my knowledge in 1839—she is alive—I saw her just now outside the Court.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. How long did he live with her after her marriage? A. They have been separated so many times, I can hardly tell you—they have been separated four times I know, and he has taken her back again—she would go away with other men, and live with them, and leave her husband and child, a little boy about seven years old, behind her—he has forgiven her two are three times, and taken her back, and some short time afterwards she has left him again, and her way of life has been horrible to mention—she has behaved very badly indeed, no woman could behave worse—I did not know of the prisoner's marrying a second time—I know now he has been living some time with a second wife—he is a waterman and lighterman—I cannot find any fault in him, he is kind, ste and hard working.
ELIZABETH BRANDON QUENNELL. I was married to the prisoner on the 27th of July, 1846, and have been living with him ever since—I was single before that, and living with my mother, in Brandon-street—I had no money—I have had no child, and am not with child now.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you lived with him up to the time of this charge being made? A. Yes—I always found him good and kind—there might be as good a husband, but none better, he was a hare-working, Industrious man—I never knew him in liquor, and any time in the night, no matter what hour, he would get up to work and earn his living—it is not my intention to abandon him now—I intend still to live with him.
JOHN BAKER ( policeman) I produce copies of the two certificates of marriage—I compared them myself with the original, they are correct—( read).
Cross-examined. Q. Have you made inquiries about him in the neighbourhood? A. I have, and found him bear the character described—the character of his first wife is vary indifferent indeed.
GUILTY. Aged 30.— Strongly recommended to mercy.— Confined Four Months
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.
[trialAccount t18471025-2495]
2495. ROBERT WAITE was indicted for stealing 4 1/4 lbs. weight of cheese, value 3 s. 9 d.; and 8lbs. weight of butter, 8 s.; the property of George Pickett, his master; and ELIZABETH SYMMONDS and EDWIN PICKERING , for feloniously receiving the same; to which
WAITE pleaded GUILTY.
GEORGE PICKETT. I am a cheesemonger, at High-street, Newington-butts. Waite was my shopman—I gave him in charge, and got an order from a Magistrate to search Symmonds' house—she at first denied having goods in the house—afterwards when the policeman said, "I shall look at see"—she said, "We have some that were delivered to me yesterday, and I have not paid for them, because I wash for Robert and do for him" (meaning Waite)—she brought down a great quantity of butter and cheese, and said something to her son—he ran into the yard with two books, which he put down the watercloset—the policeman got them out—these are them ( produced)—there are entries in them to a very great amount, since the 14th of May, of the delivery of cheese and butter which I believe to be my property—they show the delivery of goods which Mrs. Pickering has received—she acknowledged they were mine—she said Robert had been in the habit of bringing goods to her house, and she had not paid him for them, because she did for him.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Is that in your deposition? A. I do not recollect, I believe I stated it to the Magistrate—Waite was not allowed to sell for me on commission—it appears he has done so—a witness whose name I do not know represented that he was allowed to do so.
JANE ELIZABETH ORTIEB. The prisoner Waite called at my house one evening to solicit my custom, saying that Mr. Pickett would allow him a commission on any person who was a good customer—I have dealt with him in that way from that time—for the first three weeks a young man from Mr. Pickett's shop brought the things, and from that time I received them of Mrs. Symmonds.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he say that Mr. Pickett had given him permission to sell on commission? A. Yes—the three first bills were Mr. Pickett's bills—he received about 4 s. a week of me.
ELEANOR BOAKES. I have been in the habit of buying things weekly of Mrs. Symmonds—I understood that she had them for bad debts in business—I was introduced to her by an old window-lady.
WILLIAM COOMBS ( policeman.) After Waite was in custody I went to the house, and asked Symmonds if she knew anything about the butter—she said, "Yes"—I said, "Do you know Robert Waite, he is in custody for robbing his master"—she said, "I have nothing in my house, and have had nothing of Mr. Pickett's"—I said, "I must search"—she said, "What I have had I have always paid for; there is some butter up stairs"—I found 8lbs. of butter, and 5 1/4lb. of cheese—I said, "Is that paid for?"—she said, "No; I wash and do for Robert, and I believe he has always accounted to Mr. Pickett"—while I was there Pickering ran out with two books, and attempted to put them into the water-butt.
JOHN CONSTABLE. I got the books out of the water-closet.
(Symmonds received a good character.)
SYMMONDS — NOT GUILTY.
PICKERING — NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18471025-2496]
2496. ROBERT WAITE was again indicted for stealing 5 1/4 lbs. weight of cheese, value 3 s. 9 d.; and 2 8/4lbs. weight of bacon, 2 s. 3 d.; the goods of George Pickett, his master: and ELIZABETH SYMMONDS and EDWARD PICKERING , for feloniously receiving the same: to which
WAITE pleaded GUILTY. Aged 30.— Transported for Seven Years.
GEORGE PICKETT. I have lost some bacon and cheese, besides that charged in the other case, altogether about 120 l. worth—I know that Mrs. Boakes received it of Mrs. Symmonds. WILLIAM COOMBS ( policeman.) I went to Mrs. Symmonds, told her I came to search her house, said that Waite was in custody for robbing his master, and asked if she had any butter and cheese there—she said she had not—I said, "Do you mean to say you never had, for he has told all about it?"—she said, "I have, but I have always paid for it"—I said, "I must go over the house"—I did so, I found no bacon.
ELBANOR BOAKES. I bought some bacon of Mrs. Symmonds—she said she was obliged to take it for a bad debt when she was in business—I have had two hams—( produced)—I have had butter, cheese, and bacon—I cannot tell to what amount—it is in the book—it may be 15 l.—I and she kept the book—she had things of me as well as I having things of her.
GEORGE PICKETT re-examined. This butter is mine—Waite had the opportunity of taking it—I charged him with robbing me, I did not say what of—he confessed it—I had not seen the bacon there—I have not spoken to him about it since—Symmonds frequently came to the shop in my absence.

[trialAccount t18471025-2499]
2499. ELIZABETH BROWN was indicted for unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. HORRY and ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
SOPHIA GRANT. I am the wife of John Grant. On the 2nd of Oct. the prisoner came into my brother's shop, and asked for a quire of paper and a stick of sealing-wax—I served her—she gave me a sovereign—I gave her change, a half-sovereign and nine shillings—I asked her to give me a penny, and I would give her a sixpence—she said she had not one—she then said, "This half-sovereign don't sound a good one"—I said, "I am quite sure it is"—she said she did it to save trouble, not to bring it back again—I said, "Let me look at it"—I took it in my hand, put on my glasses, felt very doubtful of it, and called a boy and told him to go to Mr. Field, to see if it were good—thy boy went, and brought it back again, bent double—I got it back—I am quite sure it was not the half-sovereign I had given the prisoner—mine was a good one—I had been very particular in taking it—I
had taken it the day before, and I had not another—I gave the bent halfsovereign to my brother when he came home—he gave it to the policeman—I gave the prisoner the sovereign back.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You first thought you had taken your half-sovereign from a teacher that lived opposite? A. Yes, and then I found I did not, it was a mistake of my brother's—I said I was confused, and did not know what I was about, and the prisoner might as well have taken the nine shillings, but she did not take them—I had taken my halfsovereign of a person whose face was familiar to me, but not a teacher—I have been in the jewellery business some years and am a good judge of money, but I never heard of ringing the changes before—I took my halfsovereign out of my pocket—I had had it there one day.
CHARLES MILES. I am in the service of Mr. Cockin. On the 2nd of Oct., about five o'clock in the afternoon, Mrs. Grant gave me a half-sovereign to take to Mr. Field—I took it him—he gave it me back, and I took it to Mrs. Grant.
Cross-examined. Q. What did Mr. Field do with it? A. He bent it on the counter—he did not take it into another room.
GEORGE FIELD. The boy Miles gave me a half-sovereign—I gave him the same back.
GEORGE COFFIN. I got a half-sovereign from my sister, Sophia Grant—I gave it to George Beck, the constable.
GEORGE BECK ( policeman, V 164.) I produce the half-sovereign.
MARTHA STANDNIG. My husband keeps a china-shop. On the 5th of Oct. the prisoner came and wanted a china mug, with the name of "Mary" or "Ann" on it—I looked and could not find one—she looked at the counter, and said, "I will take one of these custard-cups"—the price of it was 1 s.—she gave me a sovereign—I had no change, and went up to my cash-box, and brought down nine shillings and a half-sovereign—I did not think it was right, and I bit the sovereign before I took it up stairs, and sounded it before her—she said, "It is a good one"—I gave her the change—she was putting it away, I turned to put the custard-glass into a piece of paper, and she said, "This half-sovereign does not sound well; I think it is cracked"—I said, "Let me see it"—she hesitated for a moment, and I asked her again—she gave it me, and said, "It is a cracked one"—I took it from her hand, and said, "It is a very base one"—I bit it double, and said, "I am confident this is not the one I gave you"—she said directly, "Oh, my good soul, what could I have done with the other!"—I said, "I don't know; I am confident this is not the one I gave you," and directly she faltered, and said, "If you will give it me, let me see it; I will take it to my father, and he shall see it, or I will cut it in two before him"—I did not allow her to go—she said, rather than there should be a bother she would leave the shop.
WILLIAM COTTON ( policeman, F 52.) I took the prisoner—this halfsovereign was given me by Mrs. Stevens.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not the prisoner say she had no other halfsovereign, it was the same the lady gave her? A. Yes—this was marked before it was given me.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL. I am inspector of coin to the Royal Mint. These half-sovereigns are both counterfeit, and both cast in one mould—they have been cast first in white metal and then galvanized.
GUILTY. Aged 20.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18471025-2500]
2500. ALFRED DAWSON was indicted for a like offence.
MESSRS. HORRY and WILDE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM DAVIES. I keep the Queen's Head, at Mortlake. On the 22nd of Sept. I served the prisoner some brandy—he tendered me a half-crown in payment—I bit it, and found it was bad—it was put into the fire by the prisoner's request—he went away, and I followed him, and saw him in Mr. Willis's shop.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You said you believed it to be Alfred Dawson? A. Yes—he was much surprised when I told him the half-crown was bad—he gave me shillings, and said he was sorry such a thing should happen, and was not aware he had it about him—he did not stay more than five minutes at my house—he staid till the half-crown was melted—I showed him the pieces.
MR. WILDE. Q. Were you sent for by Mr. Day? A. Yes—I saw the prisoner, and said he was the same person.
WILLIAM LANGRIDGE. I keep a beer-shop. On the 22nd of Sept. the prisoner came for a glass of ale—he gave me a half-crown—I returned him 2 s. 4 d. out—he went away—I examined the half-crown in about five minutes afterwards—I am quite certain it was the same I received from him—it was bad, and I threw it on the fire—there was no fire alight—I took it out and gave it to Dumain.
Cross-examined. Q. How long did the prisoner stay at your house? A. Five or six minutes—nothing passed between him and me about the halfcrown—he had a glass of old ale—my suspicion was not at all excited by the half-crown—I took it and put it away—in consequence of something I heard afterwards I went and looked at it—I am a pork-butcher and a beer-shop keeper—I am in the habit of taking a good deal of silver.
GEORGE DAY. I keep the public-house at Mortlake. On Wednesday, the 22nd of Sept., I saw the prisoner served with some brandy by Elisabeth Day, my niece—he put her down a half-crown to pay for it—I thought it was good—I bit it, and found it was bad—he wanted me to give it him back—he said, "Let me look at it"—I said I would take care of that and him too—I sent for Mr. Davies—he said the prisoner was the same person who had been offering him a half-crown.
Cross-examined. Q. Did the prisoner bear Mr. Davies say so? A. He was quite close to him—he wanted to go to his friends—he was in my place half an hour altogether, being searched and all—after I told him it was bad he said, "Give it to me, or throw it away"—he said he did not know he had such a thing about him—he had 6 d. worth of brandy and water afterwards—if I had not heard something about it I should have taken the halfcrown as good—when the second brandy was supplied him he seemed a little fidgetty—he drank about one-third of it.
MR. HORRY. Q. Did he appear drunk? A. He appeared very sober when he came in, and afterwards seemed to be very fidgety—he said he should lose the train and omnibus—he was very anxious to get away.
JAMES DOMAN ( policeman, V 120.) On Wednesday, the 22nd of Sept., I received from Mr. Davies these pieces of melted metal, and one half-crown from Mr. Langridge, and this other from Mr. Day.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL. These pieces of white metal are the same as these two half-crowns—they are both counterfeit, but of different reigns.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 27.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18471025-2501]
2501. JAMES LEE was indicted for stealing 45lb. weight of lead pipe, value 11 s.; 1 basket, 10 d.; the goods of George Robinson, his master.
GEORGE DAY. I am foreman to Mr. George Robinson—the prisoner was in his employ. I was at work on the 4th of Oct. at Richmond, and about four o'clock in the afternoon I saw the prisoner coming out of a shed where there was lead kept—he was bringing part of a coil of lead—he placed it at the end of the shed, near a wall, where there was a path on the other side—he went back to the shed—he had no business whatever with the lead—it was my master's—he moved it about twenty yards.
THOMAS PIRIES. I went into the shed on that day and saw the prisoner bending a piece of lead backwards and forwards—I told the foreman and went back to my work—in half an hour afterwards I saw the prisoner take a roll of lead and put it behind a place on my master's premises—I am certain it is my master's lead.
GEORGE WOODS ( policeman, V 209.) I took the prisoner and have the lead—I found this basket at the prisoner's lodging.
GEORGE DAY re-examined. This basket is my master's property.

2502. ALEXANDER STORAR was indicted for stealing 1 barometer, value 10 s., the property of Robert Bastow and others.
BENJAMIN WILLMOT WHEATLAND. I am an officer of the Southwark Literary Institution. On the 18th of Sept. I missed a barometer—this is it—it is the property of Robert Bastow and others.
JAMES WALTER. I am a pawnbroker—I have a barometer I took in—I cannot say of whom—this is the duplicate I gave of it.
RICHARD MERRONY ( policeman, R 102.) I took the prisoner—I found this duplicate on him of the barometer pawned on the 18th of Sept.
Prisoner's Defence. A man named Hardy owed me some money; I asked him several times for it, and he told me to call on Saturday afternoon, and he would give me part and security for the rest; I called, he gave me a shilling, and said he had a barometer out of repair, I might pawn it; I did so; I sent my wife to the man afterwards, and he was gone away—I told the officer where I lived and where I worked.
RICHARD MERRONY re-examined. Yes, he did—I went to his masters, who said he knew nothing dishonest of him.
GUILTY. Aged 18.— Confined Six Months.

2503. SARAH GROOM was indicted for stealing 1 shawl, value 2 s., the property of Phoebe Pearce; having been before convicted of felony.
PHOEBE PEARCE. I am single, and live at Mortlake. On the 18th of Oct. I saw the prisoner at Richmond with my shawl on—I had seen it safe at home at four o'clock that afternoon—I went home, it was gone from the table where I had left it—I went back after the prisoner and gave her in charge—the policeman took the shawl from her—this is my shawl.

2506. MARY MASON, WILLIAM BROWN , and ELIZA WEAVER , were indicted for stealing 45 yards of lace, value 3 l. 9 s., the property of William Kingston; Mason having been before convicted of felony.
MR. PENDERGAST conducted the Prosecution.
RICHARD JOLLIFFE LANGDON. I am shopman to William Kingston, of Westminster-bridge-road—on Wednesday, the 22nd of Sept., at half-past three in the afternoon, the prisoner Mason and another woman came to the shop and asked to look at some black edgings—I showed them some of different widths—there was some lace in the same box—I took out several cards of lace and edging, and laid them on the counter—Mason and the other woman looked at them—Mason asked for a yard and quarter of black edging—it was 8 1/2 d. a yard—I served her—I then went and got some other goods to show them—I was away a very short time—long enough for a person to go out of the shop and return without my observing it—I then sold something to the other woman—the officer came in and told the foreman not to let the women go—I think Mason heard it—the foreman then spoke to me—I looked at the goods at the door—I did not miss anything—in a quarter of an hour afterwards Laylan and I went to the station—I there saw three cards, containing 48 yards of lace, worth about 3 l. 9 s.—I knew them to be my master's property—I had had it out while Mason and the other woman were there—this is it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Has your master any partner? A. No—the two women bought separate things—they came in at the same time, and went out the same time—I did not miss anything—I did not see one of them go out—I know this from the marks on it—it was on the counter while they were in the shop—I took it out of the box—I looked at the goods at the door, as the foreman understood it was from there, and did not miss anything—I did not search amongst the property on the counter—there were perhaps twenty or thirty cards of lace in the box—I know this was not sold, we never sell it so—if these had been sold, we should probably have put them on one card.
Cross-examined by MR. PARTINGER. Q. You only speak to two women being in the shop? A. No.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Were these cards of lace part of what you had in the box? A. Yes, I have no doubt they were—I had a quantity of this sort and width—while I was gone to the station our people found we had lost lace of this description—I have not looked in the box myself to see whether any of this was gone—we cut lace in lengths—we sell it with our
private marks on the cards—nobody but me served the women with lace—I did not serve them with this—I produced such lace as this while they were there, but did not serve any of this to Mason or the other woman—neither of them bought this.
JONATHAN WILLCHER. ( police-sergeant, 27 A.) On Wednesday afternoon, the 22nd of Sept., I saw the prisoners and another woman in Westminster-road—Mason and the other woman went into Mr. Harvey's shop—they left there, and went to Mr. Kingston's—Mason and the other woman went in—the other prisoners remained outside, near the shop-door—I saw Mason or the other woman, I am not certain which, but I believe Mason, come out and go to Weaver, and, as it appeared to me, give her something, which Weaver put under her shawl—the woman then returned to the shop directly, and Weaver and Brown went down Phœnix-street, which adjoins Mr. Kingston's shop—I told Langley what I had seen, went into the shop, and saw Mason and the other woman sitting at the counter—I told the foreman I was an officer, and I gave him instructions to retain the women, which it seems they did not attend to—I then came out and joined Langdon—we went down Phœnix-street, and about 200 yards down, Brown and Weaver were standing talking—I said to Weaver, "What have you got under your shawl?"—she said, "Nothing"—I lifted up her shawl, and found three cards of lace under her arm—I said, "What do you call these?"—she said she picked them up in the street—I took hold of Brown, and said he must go to the station—Langley took hold of Weaver—I had seen the prisoners together about twenty minutes before they went to the shop—they were all walking together—at the time Mason or the other woman brought something out, and gave to Weaver, Brown was close by, within two or three yards of the window—it is a corner house—he might be half a dozen yards from the door—I found on Weaver a purse, nine shillings, and a penny, four or five pieces or ribbon, two small bits of lace, and pair of child's socks, all new—I went with Langley about three hours afterwards to the Swan public-house in Bishopsgate-street, saw Mason there, and told her I apprehended her for taking some lace that afternoon in Westminster-road—she said I was mistaken, she had not been in Westminster-road, nor on that side the water—I am certain she is the person—I know her well—there was a new scarf found on Brown—this is it.
Cross-examined by MR. PARTINGER. Q. When you took Brown, he said he knew nothing of the robbery? A. Yes; he said before the Magistrate that he accidentally met Weaver.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Where were you standing? A. passed them first on the same side of the way—I then went over the way to watch them—there is a pawnbroker's shop at the opposite corner to Mr. Kingston's—Brown and the woman not in custody live as man and wife.
EDWARD LANGLEY ( police-serjeant, A 25.) I was with Whicher, in Westminster-road, on Wednesday the 22nd of Sept., and I saw the three prisoners and another—I watched them down the Westminster-road, from Mr. Harvey's shop to Mr. Kingston's, which is more than 100 yards—I saw Brown and Weaver remain outside, Mason and the other woman went into Mr. Kingston's shop, and remained some time—Brown and Weaver remained outside—they then went down the street—Whitaker and I went down, and asked Weaver what she had under her shawl—she said, "Nothing"—we lifted her shawl, and took from under her right arm the three cards of lace—I searched Brown, and found on him this scarf, a small knife, and this duplicate
of a ring—I afterwards went to the public-house and took Mason—she was told what she was taken for—she said she had never been on that side the water.
THOMAS GRUBEY. I am apprentice to Mr. Kingston. I was in the shop when the two women came in for some lace—Langdon served them—while he was away I saw one of the women, who I believe was Mason, leave the shop and came back.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Was it not the shorter one of the two whom you believed was Mason? A. Yes—she is shorter than the other was.
HENRY MERVYN ( policeman, F 139.) I produce a certificate of Mason's former conviction, by the name of Mary Rode—( Read — Convicted Aug. 1845, confined four months)—she is the person.
(Brown and Weaver received good characters.)
MASON — GUILTY. Aged 23.— Transported for Seven Years.
BROWN — GUILTY. Aged 34.— Confined Nine Months.
WEAVER — GUILTY. Aged 27.— Confined Nine Months.

JOHN SMITH. I am in the employ of Mr. Wright. On the 10th of Oct. I saw Burkett standing at the horse's head—he beckoned to Raymond, and showed him something that was in the riding-box of the cart—Raymond went away and beckoned, and then Gainor came and stopped with them five minutes—then Raymond and Harrington came up—Gainor got on the step of the cart, and got two 5 s. papers of copper—he gave them to Harrington—I went after Harrington and got one paper back—the others went away.

2509. ELIZABETH CORNWALL was indicted for stealing 12 sovereigns, the moneys of Maria Bromley.
MARIA BROMLEY. I live in Frederick-place, Old Kent-road. The prisoner was occasionally staying with me—on the 13th of Aug. I put twelve sovereigns
into my chest—I have not seen them since—the prisoner was there on the 14th—I missed them two or three weeks afterwards—the chest was locked, but the prisoner frequently borrowed my keys—no one else borrowed them—the prisoner borrowed 5 s. of me—she said she had no money, and very few clothes—I said she must go and seek a situation.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. She had a box there? A. Yes—I do not know that one of my keys opened it—her key was at the bottom of a large box—she borrowed my friend's keys as well as mine—she said they would not do, and borrowed mine the same day, and several times afterwards—I know she was there on the 14th, because I had a friend there that day—she said if I would go with her to Islington I should he paid back all the money I had lost—I have had no quarrel with her—I keep a bonnet-stop—this money I brought with me to pay a baker's bill.
ELLEN FRENCH. The prisoner came to me in July, and with me up to the 13th of Sept.—I was she had six or seven sovereigns and some silver.
Cross-examined. Q. How was this money shown to you? A. I was standing at the corner of the table, and she took her purse, and paid me for an article I purchased for her—she did not appear timid in showing the gold.
ANN FITZGLIN. The prisoner came to live with me in Little Qucen-street—the policeman came there look in the drawers—the prisoner pointed with her finger—I could not understand what she meant—she then said to me, "Say it is yours"—the officer was then looking at a brown paper parcel—I had seen the prisoner with some silver the night before, but no gold.
Cross-examined. Q. Did the policeman hear what she said? A. Yes—he said to me, "Is this yours?"—I hesitated about it, and then said, "Yes"—he said, "Are you sure?"—I then said, "No"—I was rather confused—she appeared quite calm, and spoke to the officer as if he were a friend.
WILLIAM MORTEN ( police-sergeant, T 7.) I took prisoner—I charged her with stealing 12 l.—she said, "I stole no money"—I asked for her pocket—she gave me this purse, which contained some silver—I said, "You had some money"—she said, "Who said so?"—I said, "Mrs. French"—she then said she had 10 l. given her by a gentleman she had lived with, and there was a letter in her box would show it—I then searched a drawer and found a parcel—I saw her make a sign to Miss Fitzglin—I found a dress and a number of other things in the parcel, all new—I told her I understood she bought those things after leaving Mr. Bromley's—she said, yes, but most of them were presents—I took her to the station—she said, "I have not stolen Mrs. Bromley's money, but if you will go to Islington the money will be paid."
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18471025-2510]
2510. WILLIAM JAMES HOPKINS was indicted for stealing 40 bushels of oats, value 5 l.; the goods of John Dudin; in a barge on the Thames.
JOHN ADCOCK. I live in St. John-street, Horseleydown, and am a City deputy corn-meter. On the 4th of Oct. I went on board the barge Tennant, which was lying in the river, at Prince's-stairs, Rotherhithe—I went to do 330 quarters of oats—I delivered 260 quarters in the first barge—I saw the prisoner in the barge, acting as lighterman, in taking the oats in and stacking them—they were delivered partly in bulk and partly in sacks—I was called away from the hold after the 260 quarters had been delivered—on earning on deck I saw a skiff under the bow of the vessel; I noticed seven or eight sacks in the skiff. I saw the prisoner coming towards the moon hatch-way—that
was some distance from the skiff—I said to him, "You did not tell me you were going to take any oats in the skiff—he said, "Yes; five quarters"—I said, "It is strange you did not tell me"—he said, "It is all right"—I saw Wheeler standing at the stern of the barge—I said, it was strange that he did not tell me that he was loading the skiff—I asked Hopkins whether Wheeler worked for Mr. Dudin—he said, "Yes"—I had seen Hopkins go to him and speak to him—I saw Wheeler take the skiff and tow it towards Prince's-stairs, with the sacks in it—I made out a bill and gave it to the prisoner—he said, "You have not given me a bill for the five quarters"—I said, "No"—he said, "Don't take any notice of the five quarters, as we have slinked them"—I went and gave information to Mr. Dudin.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. The prisoner was in Mr. Dudin's employ? A. Yes; as a lighterman—Mr. Dudin is a carman and lighterman—he does not deal in corn—I know Wheeler by sight—I did not know that he was in Mr. Dudin's employ—it is usual to produce a written order for the delivery of corn; it would have been in the usual course for Wheeler to have brought a delivery order to me—he brought that order; but this was done while I was in the hold of the vessel—I saw the skiff when I was called up—the captain had charge of the Tennant —he would be the person to receive the delivery order—he is not here—he was on board at the time this was going on—I cannot tell the meaning of the word slinked —the expresion was, "You have not given me a bill for the five quarters, "I said, "No;" and he said, "Take no notice of them, as we have slinked them"—I will swear he said that—I did not take any steps to stop the barge from going off—I did not deliver a bill for the five quarters.
COURT Q. Did the bill you delivered include the five quarters? A. Yes—I delivered the oats from the vessel into the barge—I was the man to measure them, and a lighterman was taking them into the barge—being called from below, I discovered a part of what was entered for the barge had got into the skiff—if they had been put into the barge it would have been all right—they had no business in the skiff as far as I know—Wheeler went away alone with the skiff—I do not know what became of it—the prisoner stopped—I loaded another barge with seventy quarters—a lad came and took me out by a note—the note was left on board the vessel—I acted upon that—an order was left for 200, and one for 130—two sacks contain a quarter—I had not delivered the 330 quarters when this conversation took place—I had delivered seventy—the skiff went away about twelve o'clock—the conversation about not noticing the five quarters must have been about half-past eleven—I had been in the interim delivering the rest—I do not count the sacks—the bulk here was 1600 or 1700 quarters—I take my shovel, and put it into the sacks, and score with chalk on the shovel—the employ sacks are thrown down, filled, and taken away—I am responsible for the quantity, because I deliver the quantity according to the order.
JAMES ASTED. I am a fellowship-porter, and was employed in delivering the oats—I saw the skiff with several sacks in it—my attention was called to it by the captain, and I saw Wheeler walk across the plank, across the barge we were loading, and put a sack into the skiff—I called Adcock—he said to the prisoner, "Why did you not tell me you were going to load a skiff?"—he said, "It is all right"—Adcock said, "Won't you want a bill for it?"—he said, "No, you can give me that when you have loaded the barge"—I thought that Wheeler was in Mr. Dudin's service as well as the prisoner—they had been working in the Eclipse barge that we were loading, and
brought a bundles of empty sacks—a boy who had been in the skiff three or four hours was sent on shore.
JOAN DUDIN. I am a master lighterman, and live in Gainsford-street, Horseleydown. On the 4th of Oct. the prisoner was in my service—Wheeler was not—I had 330 quarter of oats to work out of the Tennant on the day in question—I directed the prisoner to load the Eclipse, and to put the rest in the Jolon barge—I gave no direction as to the use of a skiff—the bundle of sacks was to stack the Eclipse —he was to take the Eclipse to Whiting's wharf—that was not done—Adcock gave me information—I afterwards saw the barge inside the tier—I found the skiff at Prince's stairs—nothing was in it then—it was in my charge, so that the prisoner could get it before twelve o'clock—I crossed the water—I saw a boy who I employed to load the barge, go ashore—the oats were to be delivered to Edward Raper—they were delivered, but not the five quarters—I have been charged for this deficiency, and had to pay for it—I went to the Thames-police-office, and went with an officer to find the prisoner—I found him in the George public-house—I asked him what had become of the five quarters—he said he knew nothing about them.
Cross-examined. Q. Where was the Tennant lying? A. At Prince's stairs—about half a mile from Whiting's—I only employed the prisoner to unload this corn—there was a boy employed to turn the meter—the prisoner had not been long in my employ—he was merely employed by the day—that is the general practice—Wheeler is occasionally employed by persons there—that is the way he gets his living—I gave the order the night before—I have never recorded the five quarters, nor found what was done with them, nor found Wheeler.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 22.— Confined Eighteen Months.
[trialAccount t18471025-2511]
2511. SAMUEL TOOMBS was indicted for bigamy.
JOHN STRIDE. I am an engineer, and live in Victory-street, New Town, Deptford. I have know the prisoner about nine years—I was with him in Oct., about nine years ago, at the parish Church of St. George's, Bloomsbury, when he was married to Mary Woolridge—he was then about thirty or thirty-one years of age—I was one of the witnesses to the marriage—I afterwards saw them living together as man and wife for three or four years—she is now alive—I saw her five minutes ago—she is about the same age or rather older than the prisoner—I had not been there before the marriage—none of the other witnesses are here—my wife was one—I had known the female rather longer then the prisoner—the prisoner was a carpenter—I do not know the name of the elergyman who performed the ceremony—Mr. Catty was the clerk—he was present.
Cross-examined by MR. WOLLETT. Q. Do you know on what terms the prisoner lived with his wife? A. In my opinion he lived on very good terms—I do not knew of any separation—I have heard that it is three or four years ago since he left her—I then saw them living together very happily, and comfortably—there did not appear much disparity in their ages—I have heard of the prisoners allowing her so much a week.
JAMES POOR. I am a cabinet-marker, in Fulham-road, Hammersmith—I know the prisoner, and was him at St. Luke's Church, Chelsea, on last August twelvemonth, when he was married to Martha Ginger—she is not
here—I believe this prosecution is at the instance of the first wife—Martha Ginger was a single woman, about twenty-eight years old—she had no property—she was a servant.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know the prisoner? A. Yes, personally—I do not know on what terms he lived with his wife—I have not heard of any separation—I never knew the wife—I have heard that he allowed her something weekly, up to recently.
WILLAM GIBBS ( policeman, V 77.) The prisoner was given into my custody by his first wife—he told me he was very sorry, but he would go quietly with me—I have copies of the registers of the marriages—the first wife gave me this one—I examined it with the register at Bloomsbury Church, with the clerk—it is a true copy—and the other also—( read).
Cross-examined. Q. Did he say on what terms he lived with his first wife? A. No—I believe he allowed her 7 s. 6 d. a week till within the last month.
GUILTY. Aged 31.— Confined One Year.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

2512. ANN RUGG was indicted for stealing 2 sheets, value 4 s., the property of the Guardians of the Poor of St. Mary, Rotherhithe, to which she pleaded
